Venture capitalists provide this financing in the interest of generating a [[Profit (economics)|return]] through an eventual "exit" event, such as the company selling shares to the public for the first time in an [[initial public offering]] (IPO), or disposal of shares happening via a merger, via a sale to another entity such as a financial buyer in the [[private equity secondary market]] or via a sale to a trading company such as a competitor.

In addition to [[angel investing]], [[equity crowdfunding]] and other [[seed funding]] options, venture capital is attractive for new companies with limited operating history that are too small to raise [[capital (economics)|capital]] in the [[financial market|public market]]s and have not reached the point where they are able to secure a [[loan|bank loan]] or complete a [[debt|debt offering]]. In exchange for the high [[Financial risk|risk]] that venture capitalists assume by investing in smaller and early-stage companies, venture capitalists usually get significant control over company decisions, in addition to a significant portion of the companies' ownership (and consequently value). Privately owned companies who have reached a market valuation of over $1 billion are referred to as [[Unicorn (finance)|unicorns]]. As of May 2024, there were a reported total of 1248 unicorn companies.<ref>{{Cite web |title=The Complete List Of Unicorn Companies |url=https://www.cbinsights.com/research-unicorn-companies |access-date=2024-11-15 |website=instapage.cbinsights.com}}</ref> Venture capitalists also often provide strategic advice to the company's executives on its [[business model]] and marketing strategies.

==History==
===Origins of modern venture capital===
{{Main|History of venture capital}}

Before [[World War II]] (1939–1945) venture capital was primarily the domain of wealthy individuals and families. [[J.P. Morgan]], the [[Wallenberg family|Wallenbergs]], the [[Vanderbilt family|Vanderbilts]], the [[Whitney family|Whitneys]], the [[Rockefeller family|Rockefellers]], and the [[Warburg family|Warburgs]] were notable investors in private companies. In 1938, [[Laurance S. Rockefeller]] helped finance the creation of both [[Eastern Air Lines]] and [[Douglas Aircraft]], and the Rockefeller family had vast holdings in a variety of companies. [[Eric M. Warburg]] founded [[E.M. Warburg & Co.]] in 1938, which would ultimately become [[Warburg Pincus]], with investments in both [[leveraged buyouts]] and venture capital. The [[Wallenberg family]] started [[Investor AB]] in 1916 in Sweden and were early investors in several Swedish companies such as [[ABB]], [[Atlas Copco]], and [[Ericsson]] in the first half of the 20th century.

Only after 1945 did modern venture capital investment firms begin to emerge, notably with the founding of [[American Research and Development Corporation]] (ARDC) and [[J.H. Whitney & Company]] in 1946.<ref>
Wilson, John. ''The New Venturers, Inside the High Stakes World of Venture Capital.''
</ref><ref>
{{Cite book |author-last1 = Ante|author-first1= Spencer E. |title = Creative Capital: Georges Doriot and the Birth of Venture Capital |publisher = Harvard Business School Press |location = Cambridge, MA |year = 2008 |isbn = 978-1-4221-0122-3 |url-access = registration |url = https://archive.org/details/creativecapitalg00ante }}
</ref>

[[Georges Doriot]], the "father of venture capitalism",<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/theymadeamerica/whomade/doriot_hi.html|archiveurl=https://web.archive.org/web/20121208041220/http://www.pbs.org/wgbh/theymadeamerica/whomade/doriot_hi.html|url-status=dead|title=Who Made America? &#124; Innovators &#124; Georges Doriot|archivedate=December 8, 2012|website=www.pbs.org}}</ref> along with [[Ralph Flanders]] and [[Karl Compton]] (former president of [[MIT]]) founded ARDC in 1946 to encourage private-sector investment in businesses run by soldiers returning from World War II. ARDC became the first institutional private-equity investment firm to raise capital from sources other than wealthy families. Unlike most present-day venture capital firms, ARDC was a publicly traded company. ARDC's most successful investment was its 1957 funding of [[Digital Equipment Corporation]] (DEC), which would later be valued at more than $355 million after its initial public offering in 1968. This represented a return of over 1200 times its investment and an [[internal rate of return|annualized rate of return]] of 101% to ARDC.<ref>Ante, Spencer E.. Creative capital: Georges Doriot and the birth of venture capital. Boston, Harvard Business Press, 2008.</ref>

Former employees of ARDC went on to establish several prominent venture capital firms including [[Greylock Partners]], founded in 1965 by Charlie Waite and Bill Elfers; Morgan, Holland Ventures, the predecessor of Flagship Ventures, founded in 1982 by James Morgan; Fidelity Ventures, now Volition Capital, founded in 1969 by Henry Hoagland; and [[Charles River Ventures]], founded in 1970 by Richard Burnes.<ref>
Kirsner, Scott.  "Venture capital's grandfather." [[The Boston Globe]], April 6, 2008.
</ref> ARDC continued investing until 1971, when Doriot retired. In 1972 Doriot merged ARDC with [[Textron]] after having invested in over 150 companies.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Gerguri |first1=Shqipe |last2=Ramadani |first2=Veland |last3=Ibraimi |first3=Sadudin |title=Development Measures of Institutional Venture Capital in Transition Economies. The Case of Macedonia. |journal=International Journal of Academic Research in Accounting, Finance and Management Sciences |date=2012 |volume=2 |issue=4 |pages=387–398 |url=https://scholar.googleusercontent.com/scholar?q=cache:9YQmGANQgAAJ:scholar.go |access-date=30 September 2022}}</ref>

=== Early venture capital and the beginning of Silicon Valley ===
{{More citations needed section|date=January 2021}}

One of the first steps toward a professionally managed venture capital industry was the passage of the [[Small Business Investment Act of 1958]]. The 1958 Act officially allowed the U.S. [[Small Business Administration]] (SBA) to license private "Small Business Investment Companies" (SBICs) to help the financing and management of the small entrepreneurial businesses in the United States.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.sba.gov/aboutsba/sbaprograms/inv/index.html |title=Small Business Administration |access-date=2012-05-18 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20101218180213/http://www.sba.gov/aboutsba/sbaprograms/inv/index.html |archive-date=December 18, 2010 |df=mdy-all }} Small Business Administration Investment Division (SBIC)</ref> The Small Business Investment Act of 1958 provided tax breaks that helped contribute to the rise of private-equity firms.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Dilger |first1=Robert J. |title=SBA Small Business Investment CompanyProgram |url=https://fas.org/sgp/crs/misc/R41456.pdf |website=Congressional Research Service |access-date=8 July 2019 |page=1 |date=4 April 2019 |archive-date=February 7, 2020 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200207201434/https://fas.org/sgp/crs/misc/R41456.pdf |url-status=live }}</ref>

During the 1950s, putting a venture capital deal together may have required the help of two or three other organizations to complete the transaction. It was a business that was growing very rapidly, and as the business grew, the transactions grew exponentially.<ref>Lea, Charles. "Tape 4- Charles Lea". Interview by Charles Rudnick. ''National Venture Capital Association Venture Capital Oral History Project Funded by Charles W. Newhall III''. September 18, 2008.</ref> [[Arthur Rock]], one of the pioneers of Silicon Valley during his venturing the [[Fairchild Semiconductor]] is often credited with the introduction of the term "venture capitalist" that has since become widely accepted.<ref>{{Cite web |last=School |first=Stanford Law |title=Legal Matters: Arthur Rock on the Early Venture Capital Decisions That Sparked Decades of Innovation |url=https://law.stanford.edu/stanford-lawyer/articles/legal-matters-arthur-rock-on-the-early-venture-capital-decisions-that-sparked-decades-of-innovation/ |access-date=2023-01-28 |website=Stanford Law School |date=January 5, 2023 |language=en |archive-date=January 28, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230128183355/https://law.stanford.edu/stanford-lawyer/articles/legal-matters-arthur-rock-on-the-early-venture-capital-decisions-that-sparked-decades-of-innovation/ |url-status=live }}</ref>

During the 1960s and 1970s, venture capital firms concentrated their investments mainly on launching and growing companies. These companies frequently capitalized on breakthroughs in electronic, medical, or data-processing technologies. As a result, venture capital came to be almost synonymous with financing of technology ventures. An early West Coast venture capital company was Draper and Johnson Investment Company, formed in 1962<ref>{{cite web |url = http://www.draperco.com/history.html |title = www.draperco.com |url-status=dead|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111002093836/http://www.draperco.com/history.html |archive-date=October 2, 2011 |df=mdy-all }}</ref> by [[William Henry Draper III]] and Franklin P. Johnson Jr. In 1965, [[Sutter Hill Ventures]] acquired the portfolio of Draper and Johnson as a founding action.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Riddell |first1=Lindsay |title=Longtime venture capitalist focuses on the person at the top |url=https://www.bizjournals.com/sanjose/stories/2007/05/28/focus4.html |website=bizjournals.com |publisher=Silicon Valley Business Journal |access-date=29 March 2023 |language=en |date=May 27, 2007 |archive-date=March 8, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230308124812/https://www.bizjournals.com/sanjose/stories/2007/05/28/focus4.html |url-status=live }}</ref> Bill Draper and Paul Wythes were the founders, and Pitch Johnson formed Asset Management Company at that time.

It was also in the 1960s that the common form of [[private-equity fund]], still in use today, emerged. [[Private-equity firm]]s organized [[limited partnership]]s to hold investments in which the investment professionals served as [[general partner]] and the investors, who were passive [[limited partners]], put up the capital. The compensation structure, still in use today, also emerged with limited partners paying an annual management fee of 1.0–2.5% and a [[carried interest]] typically representing up to 20% of the profits of the partnership.

In the 1970s, the US Government made two significant changes fuelling growth of Venture Capital firms:<ref>{{cite web |title=The New Business Preservation Act and the Tradition of U.S. Federal Government Support for Entrepreneurs |url=https://startupsusa.org/tag/public-policy/ |website=Center for American Entrepreneurship |access-date=31 January 2026 |date=2019 }}</ref><ref>{{cite web |first1=Paul A. |last1=Gomper |title=The Rise and Fall of Venture Capital, The 1994 Newcomen Prize Essay |url=https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v023n2/p0001-p0026.pdf |website=The Business History Conference |access-date=31 January 2026 |date=1994 }}</ref><ref>{{cite web |last1=Rock |first1=Arthur |title=An Evening with Legendary Venture Capitalist Arthur Rock in Conversation with John Markoff |url=https://archive.computerhistory.org/resources/access/text/2012/05/102658253-05-01-acc.pdf |website=Computer History Museum |access-date=31 January 2026 |date=1 May 2007 }}</ref>

* ERISA (Employee Retirement Income Security Act), 1972 & 1979
* Capital Gains tax reduction from 48.5% to 20%.

The growth of the venture capital industry was fueled by the emergence of the independent investment firms on [[Sand Hill Road]], beginning with [[Kleiner Perkins]] and [[Sequoia Capital]] in 1972. Located in [[Menlo Park, California]], Kleiner Perkins, Sequoia and later venture capital firms would have access to the many [[semiconductor]] companies based in the [[Santa Clara Valley]] as well as early [[computer]] firms using their devices and programming and service companies.{{NoteTag|In 1971, a series of articles entitled "Silicon Valley USA" were published in the [[Electronic News]], a weekly trade publication, giving rise to the use of the term [[Silicon Valley]].}} [[Kleiner Perkins]] was the first venture capital firm to open an office on Sand Hill Road in 1972.<ref name="Nicholas_Page_212">{{cite book |last1=Nicholas |first1=Tom |title=VC: An American History |date=2019 |publisher=Harvard University Press |location=Cambridge |isbn=9780674240117 |page=212 |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=vymWDwAAQBAJ&pg=PA212 |access-date=September 27, 2023 |archive-date=October 5, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20231005131635/https://books.google.com/books?id=vymWDwAAQBAJ&pg=PA212 |url-status=live }}</ref>

Throughout the 1970s, a group of private-equity firms, focused primarily on venture capital investments, would be founded that would become the model for later leveraged buyout and venture capital investment firms. In 1973, with the number of new venture capital firms increasing, leading venture capitalists formed the National Venture Capital Association (NVCA). The NVCA was to serve as the [[industry trade group]] for the venture capital industry.<ref>{{cite web |url = http://www.nvca.org/ |title = National Venture Capital Association |access-date=2008-07-30 |url-status=live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20080730052236/http://www.nvca.org/ |archive-date=July 30, 2008 |df=mdy-all }} Official website of the National Venture Capital Association, the largest trade association for the venture capital industry.</ref> Venture capital firms suffered a temporary downturn in 1974, when the stock market crashed and investors were naturally wary of this new kind of investment fund.

It was not until 1978 that venture capital experienced its first major fundraising year, as the industry raised approximately $750 million. With the passage of the [[Employee Retirement Income Security Act]] (ERISA) in 1974, corporate pension funds were prohibited from holding certain risky investments including many investments in [[privately held]] companies. In 1978, the [[United States Department of Labor|US Labor Department]] relaxed certain restrictions of the ERISA, under the "[[prudent man rule]]"{{NoteTag|The "prudent man rule" is a [[fiduciary responsibility]] of investment managers under ERISA. Under the original application, each investment was expected to adhere to risk standards on its own merits, limiting the ability of investment managers to make any investments deemed potentially risky.  Under the revised 1978 interpretation, the concept of portfolio diversification of risk, measuring risk at the aggregate portfolio level rather than the investment level to satisfy fiduciary standards would also be accepted.}}, thus allowing corporate pension funds to invest in the asset class and providing a major source of capital available to venture capitalists.

===1980s and VC involvement in large American companies===
The public successes of the venture capital industry in the 1970s and early 1980s (e.g., [[Digital Equipment Corporation]], [[Apple Inc.]], [[Genentech]]) gave rise to a major proliferation of venture capital investment firms. From just a few dozen firms at the start of the decade, there were over 650 firms by the end of the 1980s, each searching for the next major "home run". The number of firms multiplied, and the capital managed by these firms increased from $3 billion to $31 billion over the course of the decade.<ref name=VCVigor>POLLACK, ANDREW.  "[https://query.nytimes.com/gst/fullpage.html?res=950DE0D61E3CF93BA35753C1A96F948260 Venture Capital Loses Its Vigor] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20201027152820/https://www.nytimes.com/1989/10/08/business/venture-capital-loses-its-vigor.html |date=October 27, 2020 }}." [[New York Times]], October 8, 1989.</ref>

The growth of the industry was hampered by sharply declining returns, and certain venture firms began posting losses for the first time. In addition to the increased competition among firms, several other factors affected returns. The market for initial public offerings cooled in the mid-1980s before collapsing after the stock market crash in 1987, and foreign corporations, particularly from [[Japan]] and [[Korea]], flooded early-stage companies with capital.<ref name="VCVigor"/>

In response to the changing conditions, corporations that had sponsored in-house venture investment arms, including [[General Electric]] and [[Paine Webber]] either sold off or closed these venture capital units. Additionally, venture capital units within [[Chemical Banking|Chemical Bank]] and [[Continental Illinois National Bank and Trust Company|Continental Illinois National Bank]], among others, began shifting their focus from funding early stage companies toward investments in more mature companies. Even industry founders [[J.H. Whitney & Company]] and [[Warburg Pincus]] began to transition toward [[leveraged buyouts]] and [[growth capital]] investments.<ref name="VCVigor"/><ref>Kurtzman, Joel.  "{{Deprecated archive|url=https://archive.today/20120715070212/http://query.nytimes.com/gst/fullpage.html?res=940DE0DB1E3EF934A15750C0A96E948260}} PROSPECTS; Venture Capital." [[New York Times]], March 27, 1988.</ref><ref>LUECK, THOMAS J.  "{{Deprecated archive|url=https://archive.today/20120713001915/http://query.nytimes.com/gst/fullpage.html?res=9B0DE4DD113EF935A35751C0A961948260}} High Tech's Glamour fades for some venture capitalists." [[New York Times]], February 6, 1987.</ref>

===Venture capital boom and the Internet Bubble===
By the end of the 1980s, venture capital returns were relatively low, particularly in comparison with their emerging [[leveraged buyout]] cousins, due in part to the competition for hot startups, excess supply of IPOs and the inexperience of many venture capital fund managers. Growth in the venture capital industry remained limited throughout the 1980s and the first half of the 1990s, increasing from $3 billion in 1983 to just over $4 billion more than a decade later in 1994.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://reactionwheel.net/2015/01/80s-vc.html |title=Heat Death: Venture Capital in the 1980s |last=Neumann |first=Jerry |date=January 8, 2015 |website=Reaction Wheel |access-date=May 5, 2020 |archive-date=April 28, 2020 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200428191726/http://reactionwheel.net/2015/01/80s-vc.html |url-status=live }}</ref>

The advent of the [[World Wide Web]] in the early 1990s reinvigorated venture capital as investors saw companies with huge potential being formed. [[Netscape]] and [[Amazon (company)]] were founded in 1994, and [[Yahoo!]] in 1995. All were funded by venture capital. [[Internet]] IPOs—AOL in 1992; Netcom in 1994; UUNet, Spyglass and Netscape in 1995; Lycos, Excite, Yahoo!, CompuServe, Infoseek, C/NET, and E*Trade in 1996; and Amazon, ONSALE, Go2Net, N2K, NextLink, and SportsLine in 1997—generated enormous returns for their venture capital investors. These returns, and the performance of the companies post-IPO, caused a rush of money into venture capital, increasing the number of venture capital funds raised from about 40 in 1991 to more than 400 in 2000, and the amount of money committed to the sector from $1.5 billion in 1991 to more than $90 billion in 2000.<ref>National Science Foundation, "Science and Engineering Indicators–2002″</ref>

The bursting of the [[dot-com bubble]] in 2000 caused many venture capital firms to fail and financial results in the sector to decline.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Canderle |first1=Sebastien |title=Venture Capital: Lessons from the Dot-Com Days |url=https://blogs.cfainstitute.org/investor/2024/03/01/venture-capital-lessons-from-the-dot-com-days/ |website=CFA Institute Enterprising Investor |date=March 1, 2024 |access-date=April 8, 2026}}</ref>

===2001 private equity crash===
[[File:Nasdaq2.png|thumb|The technology-heavy [[NASDAQ Composite]] index peaked at 5,048 in March 2000, reflecting the high point of the dot-com bubble.]]
The [[Nasdaq]] crash and technology slump that started in March 2000 shook virtually the entire venture capital industry as valuations for startup technology companies collapsed. Over the next two years, many venture firms had been forced to write-off large proportions of their investments, and many funds were significantly "[[Under water (loan)|under water]]" (the values of the fund's investments were below the amount of capital invested). Venture capital investors sought to reduce the size of commitments they had made to venture capital funds, and, in numerous instances, investors sought to unload existing commitments for cents on the dollar in the [[private equity secondary market|secondary market]]. By mid-2003, the venture capital industry had shriveled to about half its 2001 capacity. Nevertheless, [[PricewaterhouseCoopers|PricewaterhouseCoopers']] MoneyTree Survey<ref>{{cite web |url = http://www.pwcmoneytree.com/moneytree/index.jsp |title = MoneyTree Survey |publisher = Pwcmoneytree.com |date = February 21, 2007 |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = dead |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20070614161950/http://www.pwcmoneytree.com/moneytree/index.jsp |archive-date = June 14, 2007 |df = mdy-all }}</ref> shows that total venture capital investments held steady at 2003 levels through the second quarter of 2005.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}}

Although the post-boom years represent just a small fraction of the peak levels of venture investment reached in 2000, they still represent an increase over the levels of investment from 1980 through 1995. As a percentage of GDP, venture investment was 0.058% in 1994, peaked at 1.087% (nearly 19 times the 1994 level) in 2000 and ranged from 0.164% to 0.182% in 2003 and 2004. The revival of an [[Internet]]-driven environment in 2004 through 2007 helped to revive the venture capital environment. However, as a percentage of the overall private-equity market, venture capital has still not reached its mid-1990s level, let alone its peak in 2000.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}}

Venture capital funds, which were responsible for much of the fundraising volume in 2000 (the height of the [[dot-com bubble]]), raised only $25.1 billion in 2006, a 2% decline from 2005 and a significant decline from its peak.<ref>Dow Jones Private Equity Analyst as referenced in Taub, Stephen.  {{cite web |url=http://www.cfo.com/article.cfm/8537972/c_8519925?f=home_todayinfinance |title=Record Year for Private Equity Fundraising - - CFO.com |access-date=2008-06-08 |url-status=dead |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110614003624/http://www.cfo.com/article.cfm/8537972/c_8519925?f=home_todayinfinance |archive-date=June 14, 2011 |df=mdy-all }} Record Year for Private Equity Fundraising.  CFO.com, January 11, 2007.</ref> The decline continued till their fortunes started to turn around in 2010 with $21.8 billion invested (not raised).<ref>{{cite web |url=https://venturebeat.com/2011/01/20/vcs-boom-in-2010/ |title=VCs roared back in 2010, investing the most money since 2007 |work=VentureBeat |date=January 21, 2011 |access-date=2021-09-17 |archive-date=September 17, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210917085201/https://venturebeat.com/2011/01/20/vcs-boom-in-2010/ |url-status=live }}</ref> The industry continued to show phenomenal growth and in 2020 hit $80 billion in fresh capital.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://pitchbook.com/news/articles/vc-is-better-than-ever-why-so-few-first-time-funds |title=VC is performing better than ever. Then why are so few first-time funds being raised? |date=May 4, 2021 |access-date=2021-09-17 |archive-date=September 17, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210917085515/https://pitchbook.com/news/articles/vc-is-better-than-ever-why-so-few-first-time-funds |url-status=live }}</ref> Again, in 2022, there was a brief decline in venture capital access, but it has since recovered due to strong networks powering venture investment.<ref>{{cite news |last=Somerville |first=Heather |title=Startups End a Bruising 2022, Stare Down Another Challenging Year |newspaper=The Wall Street Journal |date=January 1, 2023 |url=https://www.wsj.com/articles/startups-end-a-bruising-2022-stare-down-another-challenging-year-11672527313}}
</ref>

==Financing==
{{main|Venture capital financing}}

Obtaining venture capital is substantially different from raising debt or a loan. Lenders have a legal right to interest on a loan and repayment of the capital irrespective of the success or failure of a business. Venture capital is invested in exchange for an equity stake in the business. The return of the venture capitalist as a shareholder depends on the growth and profitability of the business. This return is generally earned when the venture capitalist "exits" by selling its shareholdings when the business is sold to another owner.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Venture Capitalists |url=https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/capital-markets/venture-capitalists/ |access-date=2023-02-11 |website=Corporate Finance Institute |language=en-US |archive-date=February 11, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230211183834/https://corporatefinanceinstitute.com/resources/capital-markets/venture-capitalists/ |url-status=live }}</ref>

Venture capitalists are typically very selective in deciding what to invest in, with a Stanford survey of venture capitalists revealing that 100 companies were considered for every company receiving financing.<ref>{{Cite news|title=How Do Venture Capitalists Make Decisions?|work=Stanford University Graduate School of Business Research Paper|df=mdy-all|ssrn = 2801385}}</ref> Ventures receiving financing must demonstrate an excellent management team, a large potential market, and most importantly high growth potential, as only such opportunities are likely capable of providing financial returns and a successful exit within the required time frame (typically 8–12 years) that venture capitalists expect.<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://pitchbook.com/news/articles/the-venture-capital-lifecycle|title=The Venture Capital Lifecycle - PitchBook |date=May 14, 2014 }}</ref>

Because investments are [[Market liquidity|illiquid]] and require the extended time frame to harvest, venture capitalists are expected to carry out detailed [[due diligence]] prior to investment. Venture capitalists also are expected to nurture the companies in which they invest, in order to increase the likelihood of reaching an [[Initial public offering|IPO]] stage when [[Valuation (finance)|valuations]] are favourable. Venture capitalists typically assist at four stages in the company's development:<ref>[http://www.banknetindia.com/finance/vcapital.htm Investment philosophy of VCs] {{webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20080612195436/http://www.banknetindia.com/finance/vcapital.htm |date=June 12, 2008 }}.</ref>
* [[Business idea|Idea generation]];
* [[Startup company|Start-up]];
* [[Ramp-up]]; and
* [[Exit strategy#In business|Exit]]

Because there are no public exchanges listing their securities, private companies meet venture capital firms and other private-equity investors in several ways, including warm referrals from the investors' trusted sources and other business contacts; investor conferences and symposia; and summits where companies pitch directly to investor groups in face-to-face meetings, including a variant known as "Speed Venturing", which is akin to speed-dating for capital, where the investor decides within 10 minutes whether he wants a follow-up meeting. In addition, some new private online networks are emerging to provide additional opportunities for meeting investors.<ref name="news.bbc.co.uk">{{Cite news |date=2010-05-12 |title=Cash-strapped entrepreneurs get creative |url=https://www.bbc.com/news/10100885 |access-date=2025-07-21 |work=BBC News |language=en-GB}}</ref>

This need for high returns makes venture funding an expensive capital source for companies, and most suitable for businesses having large up-front [[capital requirement]]s, which cannot be financed by cheaper alternatives such as debt. That is most commonly the case for intangible assets such as software, and other intellectual property, whose value is unproven. In turn, this explains why venture capital is most prevalent in the fast-growing [[high tech|technology]] and [[life sciences]] or [[biotechnology]] fields.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Q3 2022 venture capital investment trends |url=https://www.ey.com/en_us/growth/venture-capital/q3-2022-venture-capital-investment-trends |access-date=2023-02-11 |website=www.ey.com |language=en-US |archive-date=February 11, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230211183833/https://www.ey.com/en_us/growth/venture-capital/q3-2022-venture-capital-investment-trends |url-status=live }}</ref>

If a company does have the qualities venture capitalists seek including a substantial business plan, a good management team, investment and passion from the founders, a potential to exit the investment before the end of their funding cycle, and target minimum returns in excess of 40% per year, it will find it easier to raise venture capital.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}}

===Financing stages===
There are multiple stages of [[venture round|venture]] financing offered in venture capital, that roughly correspond to these stages of a company's development.<ref>''Corporate Finance'', 8th Edition. Ross, Westerfield, Jaffe. McGraw-Hill publishing, 2008.</ref>
* Pre-seed funding: The earliest round of financing needed to prove a new idea, often provided by friends and family, [[angel investor]]s, [[startup accelerator]]s, and sometimes by venture capital funds. [[Equity crowdfunding]] is also emerging as an option for seed funding.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Series A, B, C, D, and E Funding: How It Works |url=https://www.startups.com/library/expert-advice/series-funding-a-b-c-d-e |access-date=2023-02-17 |website=www.startups.com |language=en |archive-date=February 17, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230217043029/https://www.startups.com/library/expert-advice/series-funding-a-b-c-d-e |url-status=live }}</ref>
* Early Stage: Early stage funding includes [[Seed money|Seed]] and [[Series A round|Series A]] financing rounds. Companies use this capital to find [[Product/market fit|product-market fit]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Westaway |first=Kyle |title=Learn The Five Most Important Terms In A Series A Funding Round |url=https://www.forbes.com/sites/kylewestaway/2023/01/05/learn-the-five-most-important-terms-in-a-series-a-funding-round/ |access-date=2023-02-17 |website=Forbes |language=en |archive-date=February 17, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20230217043029/https://www.forbes.com/sites/kylewestaway/2023/01/05/learn-the-five-most-important-terms-in-a-series-a-funding-round/ |url-status=live }}</ref>
* [[Growth capital|Growth Capital]]: Once companies have found [[Product/market fit|product-market fit]], companies will use growth capital to scale the business. These are typically larger financing rounds with have higher valuations because the companies have started to prove traction and de-risk the investment. Growth capital typically includes Series B, Series C, and later rounds. 
* Exit of venture capitalist: VCs can exit through secondary sale or an [[initial public offering]] (IPO) or an acquisition. Early stage VCs may exit in later rounds when new investors (VCs or private-equity investors) buy the shares of existing investors. Sometimes a company very close to an IPO may allow some VCs to exit and instead new investors may come in hoping to profit from the IPO.
* Bridge financing is when a startup seeks funding in between full VC rounds. The objective is to raise a smaller amount of money to "bridge" the gap when current funds are expected to run out prior to planned future funding, intended to meet short-term working capital needs.<ref>{{Cite web|last=Mitchell|first=Cory|date=October 28, 2020|title=Bridge Financing|url=https://www.investopedia.com/terms/b/bridgefinancing.asp|access-date=January 26, 2022|website=Investopedia|archive-date=January 26, 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220126205058/https://www.investopedia.com/terms/b/bridgefinancing.asp|url-status=live}}</ref>

In early stage and growth stage financings, venture-backed companies may also seek to take [[venture debt]].<ref>{{cite web |url = http://bootlaw.com/2010/06/14/up-up-and-away-should-you-be-thinking-about-venture-debt/ |title = Bootlaw&nbsp;– Essential law for startups and emerging tech businesses&nbsp;– Up, Up and Away. Should You be Thinking About Venture Debt? |publisher = Bootlaw.com |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = dead |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20120420150031/http://bootlaw.com/2010/06/14/up-up-and-away-should-you-be-thinking-about-venture-debt/ |archive-date = April 20, 2012 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

==Firms and funds==
===Venture capitalist===
A '''venture capitalist''', or sometimes simply called a '''capitalist''', is a person who makes capital investments in companies in exchange for an [[Equity (finance)|equity stake]]. The venture capitalist is often expected to bring managerial and technical expertise, as well as capital, to their investments. A venture capital fund refers to a [[Investment fund|pooled investment]] vehicle (in the United States, often an [[Limited partnership|LP]] or [[Limited liability company|LLC]]) that primarily invests the [[financial capital]] of third-party investors in enterprises that are too risky for the standard [[capital market]]s or [[loan|bank loans]]. These funds are typically managed by a venture capital firm, which often employs individuals with technology backgrounds (scientists, researchers), business training and/or deep industry experience.<ref>{{Cite book |last=Ryan |first=Phillip |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=bjQFO8XUmVgC&q=venture+capitalist+all+you+need+to+know |title=How Venture Capital Works |date=2012-07-01 |publisher=The Rosen Publishing Group, Inc |isbn=978-1-4488-6795-0 |pages=16 |language=en |access-date=September 15, 2022 |archive-date=April 28, 2024 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240428100250/https://books.google.com/books?id=bjQFO8XUmVgC&q=venture+capitalist+all+you+need+to+know#v=snippet&q=venture%20capitalist%20all%20you%20need%20to%20know&f=false |url-status=live }}</ref>

A core skill within VCs is the ability to identify novel or disruptive technologies that have the potential to generate high commercial returns at an early stage. By definition, VCs also take a role in managing entrepreneurial companies at an early stage, thus adding skills as well as capital, thereby differentiating VC from buy-out private equity, which typically invest in companies with proven revenue, and thereby potentially realizing much higher rates of returns. Inherent in realizing abnormally high rates of returns is the risk of losing all of one's investment in a given startup company. As a consequence, most venture capital investments are done in a pool format, where several investors combine their investments into one large fund that invests in many different startup companies. By investing in the pool format, the investors are spreading out their risk to many different investments instead of taking the chance of putting all of their money in one start up firm.{{citation needed|date=August 2026}}

===Structure===
[[File:Venture Capital Fund Diagram.png|Structure of a generic venture capital fund|frame]]
{{clear}}
Venture capital firms are typically structured as [[partnerships]], the [[general partners]] of which serve as the managers of the firm and will serve as investment advisors to the venture capital funds raised. Venture capital firms in the United States may also be structured as [[limited liability company|limited liability companies]], in which case the firm's managers are known as managing members. Investors in venture capital funds are known as [[limited partner]]s. This constituency comprises both high-net-worth individuals and institutions with large amounts of available capital, such as state and private [[pension fund]]s, university [[financial endowment]]s, foundations, [[insurance]] companies, and [[Collective investment scheme|pooled investment]] vehicles, called [[Fund of funds|funds of funds]].<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.researchgate.net/publication/280083626|title=The interplay of track record and trustworthiness in venture capital fundraising|author-first1=Andreas |author-last1=Kuckertz|date=December 1, 2015|work=ResearchGate|url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150927114350/http://www.researchgate.net/publication/280083626_The_interplay_of_track_record_and_trustworthiness_in_venture_capital_fundraising|archive-date=September 27, 2015|df=mdy-all}}</ref>

===Types===
Venture capitalist firms differ in their motivations<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Röhm |first1=Patrick |last2=Köhn |first2=Andreas |last3=Kuckertz |first3=Andreas |last4=Dehnen |first4=Hermann S. |date=May 2018 |title=A world of difference? The impact of corporate venture capitalists' investment motivation on startup valuation |journal=Journal of Business Economics |language=en |volume=88 |issue=3–4 |pages=531–557 |doi=10.1007/s11573-017-0857-5 |issn=0044-2372|doi-access=free }}</ref> and approaches. There are multiple factors, and each firm is different.

Venture capital funds are generally three in types:<ref>{{Cite book |last1=Gravagna |first1=Nicole |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=vQVwAAAAQBAJ&q=types+of+investors+venture+capital+angel+investor |title=Venture Capital For Dummies |last2=Adams |first2=Peter K. |date=2013-08-15 |publisher=John Wiley & Sons |isbn=978-1-118-78470-9 |language=en |access-date=September 15, 2022 |archive-date=April 28, 2024 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20240428100043/https://books.google.com/books?id=vQVwAAAAQBAJ&q=types+of+investors+venture+capital+angel+investor#v=snippet&q=types%20of%20investors%20venture%20capital%20angel%20investor&f=false |url-status=live }}</ref>

# Angel investors 
# Financial VCs 
# Strategic VCs

Some of the factors that influence VC decisions include:
* Business situation: Some VCs tend to invest in new, disruptive ideas, or fledgling companies. Others prefer investing in established companies that need support to go public or grow.
* Some invest solely in certain industries.
* Some prefer operating locally while others will operate nationwide or even globally.
* VC expectations can often vary. Some may want a quicker public sale of the company or expect fast growth. The amount of help a VC provides can vary from one firm to the next. There are also estimates on how big of an exit a VC will expect for the company (i.e. if the size of the VC fund is $20M, estimate that they will at least want the company to exit for the size of the fund).<ref>{{cite journal|url=https://hbr.org/1986/05/how-much-money-does-your-new-venture-need|title=How Much Money Does Your New Venture Need?|journal=Harvard Business Review|date=May 1986|last1=Stancill|first1=James Mcneill|access-date=February 28, 2021|archive-date=December 9, 2020|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20201209040757/https://hbr.org/1986/05/how-much-money-does-your-new-venture-need|url-status=live}}</ref>

===Roles===
Within the venture capital industry, the general partners and other investment professionals of the venture capital firm are often referred to as "venture capitalists" or "VCs". Typical career backgrounds vary, but, broadly speaking, venture capitalists come from either an operational or a finance background. Venture capitalists with an operational background ([[operating partner]]) tend to be former founders or executives of companies similar to those which the partnership finances or will have served as management consultants. Venture capitalists with finance backgrounds tend to have [[investment banking]] or other [[corporate finance]] experience.

Although the titles are not entirely uniform from firm to firm, other positions at venture capital firms include:

{| class="wikitable"
|-
!Position
!Role
|-
|General Partners or GPs
|They run the Venture Capital firm and make the investment decisions on behalf of the fund. GPs typically put in personal capital up to 1–2% of the VC Fund size to show their commitment to the LPs.
|-
|Venture partners
|Venture partners are not employees of the firm but are expected to source potential investment opportunities ("bring in deals") and typically are compensated only for those deals with which they are involved.
|-
|Principal
|This is a mid-level investment professional position, and often considered a "partner-track" position. Principals will have been promoted from a senior associate position or who have commensurate experience in another field, such as [[investment banking]], [[management consulting]], or a market of particular interest to the strategy of the venture capital firm.
|-
|Associate
|This is typically the most junior apprentice position within a venture capital firm. After a few successful years, an associate may move up to the "senior associate" position and potentially principal and beyond. Associates will often have worked for 1–2 years in another field, such as [[investment banking]] or [[management consulting]].
|-
|Analyst
|In some cases, a venture capital firm may offer an analyst role for fresh graduates who have no prior relevant experience. 
|-
|Entrepreneur-in-residence
|Entrepreneurs-in-residence (EIRs) are experts in a particular industry sector (e.g., biotechnology or social media) and perform [[due diligence]] on potential deals. EIRs are hired by venture capital firms temporarily (6 to 18 months) and are expected to develop and pitch startup ideas to their host firm, although neither party is bound to work with each other. Some EIRs move on to executive positions within a portfolio company.
|-
|Platform Team
|Responsible for providing support and resources to portfolio companies beyond financial investment. This team focuses on offering strategic guidance, operational expertise, and access to networks to help portfolio companies scale and succeed. Platform teams may assist with talent acquisition, marketing and branding, product development, [[business networking]], and strategic partnerships.
|}

===Structure of the funds===
The average maturity of most [[private-equity fund|venture capital funds]] ranges from 10 years to 12 years, with the possibility of a few years of extensions to allow for private companies still seeking liquidity. The investing cycle for most funds is generally three to five years, after which the focus is managing and making follow-on investments in an existing portfolio.<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Huang |first1=Kelvin |last2=Malley |first2=Bridget |date=2022-02-14 |title=Sequoia Breaks Free From The 10-Year Cycle - ValueWalk |work=www.valuewalk.com |language=en-US }}</ref> This model was pioneered by successful funds in [[Silicon Valley]] through the 1980s to invest in technological trends broadly but only during their period of ascendance, and to cut exposure to management and marketing risks of any individual firm or its product.

In such a fund, the investors have a fixed commitment to the fund that is initially unfunded and subsequently "called down" by the venture capital fund over time as the fund makes its investments. There are substantial penalties for a limited partner (or investor) that fails to participate in a [[capital call]].<ref>{{Cite web |date=2010-11-08 |title=How VC Funds Work: Capital Commitments and Closings |url=https://www.theventurealley.com/2010/11/raising-a-fund-capital-commitments-and-closings/ |access-date=2022-04-09 |website=The Venture Alley |language=en-US |archive-date=March 31, 2022 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220331130735/https://www.theventurealley.com/2010/11/raising-a-fund-capital-commitments-and-closings/ |url-status=live }}</ref>

It can take anywhere from a month to several years for venture capitalists to raise money from limited partners for their fund. At the time when all of the money has been raised, the fund is said to be closed and the 10-year lifetime begins. Some funds have partial closes when one half (or some other amount) of the fund has been raised. The [[vintage year]] generally refers to the year in which the fund was closed and may serve as a means to stratify VC funds for comparison.

From an investor's point of view, funds can be: (1) [[private-equity fund|traditional]]—where all the investors invest with equal terms; or (2) [[asymmetric fund|asymmetric]]—where different investors have different terms. Typically asymmetry is seen in cases where investors have opposing interests, such as the need to not have unrelated business taxable income in the case of public tax-exempt investors.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Testa, Hurwitz & Thibeault, LLP |date=2004 |title=Investments in LLCs |url=https://www.vcexperts.com/buzz_articles/241 |access-date=April 9, 2022 |archive-date=January 21, 2022 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220121232838/https://www.vcexperts.com/buzz_articles/241 |url-status=live }}</ref>

=== Investment decision process ===
The decision process to fund a company is elusive. One study report in the ''[[Harvard Business Review]]''<ref name=":0">{{Cite news|last1=Gompers|first1=Paul|last2=Gornall|first2=Will|last3=Kaplan|first3=Steven N.|last4=Strebulaev|first4=Ilya A.|date=2021-03-01|title=How Venture Capitalists Make Decisions|work=Harvard Business Review|url=https://hbr.org/2021/03/how-venture-capitalists-make-decisions|access-date=2022-01-27|issn=0017-8012|archive-date=January 28, 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220128003040/https://hbr.org/2021/03/how-venture-capitalists-make-decisions|url-status=live}}</ref> states that VCs rarely use standard financial analytics.<ref name=":0"/> First, VCs engage in a process known as "generating deal flow," where they reach out to their network to source potential investments.<ref name=":0" /> The study also reported that few VCs use any type of financial analytics when they assess deals; VCs are primarily concerned about the cash returned from the deal as a multiple of the cash invested.<ref name=":0" /> According to 95% of the VC firms surveyed, VCs cite the founder or founding team as the most important factor in their investment decision.<ref name=":0" /> Other factors are also considered, including intellectual property rights and the state of the economy.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Vazirani |first1=Ashish |last2=Bhattacharjee |first2=Titas |title=Entrepreneurial Finance in the Twenty-first Century, a Review of Factors Influencing Venture Capitalist's Decision |journal=The Journal of Entrepreneurship |date=September 2021 |volume=30 |issue=2 |pages=306–335 |doi=10.1177/09713557211025654 }}</ref> Some argue that the most important thing a VC looks for in a company is high-growth.<ref>{{Cite web|last=Michelson|first=Joan|title=Female Founders Raised More VC Funding In 2021, But It's Complicated|url=https://www.forbes.com/sites/joanmichelson2/2021/12/26/female-founders-raised-more-vc-funding-in-2021-but-its-complicated/|access-date=2022-01-28|website=Forbes|language=en|archive-date=January 28, 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220128003040/https://www.forbes.com/sites/joanmichelson2/2021/12/26/female-founders-raised-more-vc-funding-in-2021-but-its-complicated/|url-status=live}}</ref>

The funding decision process has spawned bias in the form of a large disparity between the funding received by men and minority groups, such as women and people of color.<ref name="Malmström Voitkane What do they think">{{cite journal |last1=Malmström |first1=Malin |last2=Voitkane |first2=Aija |last3=Johansson |first3=Jeaneth |last4=Wincent |first4=Joakim |title=What do they think and what do they say? Gender bias, entrepreneurial attitude in writing and venture capitalists' funding decisions |journal=Journal of Business Venturing Insights |date=June 2020 |volume=13 |article-number=e00154 |doi=10.1016/j.jbvi.2019.e00154 }}</ref><ref name=":1">{{Cite news|title=Female Founders Raised Just 2% of Venture Capital Money in 2021|url=https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2022-01-11/women-founders-raised-just-2-of-venture-capital-money-last-year|access-date=2022-01-28|newspaper=Bloomberg.com|date=January 11, 2022|archive-date=March 3, 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220303200258/https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2022-01-11/women-founders-raised-just-2-of-venture-capital-money-last-year|url-status=live}}</ref><ref>{{cite journal |last1=Geiger |first1=Mark |title=A meta-analysis of the gender gap(s) in venture funding: Funder- and entrepreneur-driven perspectives |journal=Journal of Business Venturing Insights |date=June 2020 |volume=13 |article-number=e00167 |doi=10.1016/j.jbvi.2020.e00167 }}</ref> In 2021, female founders only received 2% of VC funding in the United States.<ref>{{Cite web|title=2021 All In: Female Founders in the US VC Ecosystem {{!}} PitchBook|url=https://pitchbook.com/news/reports/2021-all-in-female-founders-in-the-us-vc-ecosystem|access-date=2022-01-28|website=pitchbook.com|date=November 2021 |language=en|archive-date=January 28, 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220128003043/https://pitchbook.com/news/reports/2021-all-in-female-founders-in-the-us-vc-ecosystem|url-status=live}}</ref><ref name=":1" /> Some research studies have found that VCs evaluate women differently and are less likely to fund female founders.<ref name="Malmström Voitkane What do they think"/>

===Compensation===
{{Main|Carried interest}}
Venture capitalists are compensated through a combination of management fees and [[carried interest]] (often referred to as a "two and 20" arrangement):

{| class="wikitable"
|-
!Payment
!Implementation
|-
|Management fees
|Quarterly payments made by the limited partners to the fund's manager to pay for the firm's investment operations.<ref name=calpersdictionary>[http://www.calpers.ca.gov/index.jsp?bc=/investments/assets/equities/aim/pe-glossary.xml Private equity industry dictionary] {{webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20080505124540/http://www.calpers.ca.gov/index.jsp?bc=%2Finvestments%2Fassets%2Fequities%2Faim%2Fpe-glossary.xml |date=May 5, 2008 }}. [[CalPERS]] Alternative Investment Program</ref> In a typical venture capital fund, the general partners receive an annual management fee between 2% and 2.5% of the committed capital.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Silber |first=Jordan |date=2020-01-02 |title=Primer: Management Fees in Venture Capital Funds |url=https://thefundlawyer.cooley.com/primer-management-fees-in-venture-capital-funds/ |access-date=2022-04-09 |website=TheFundLawyer |language=en-US |archive-date=January 24, 2022 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220124105455/https://thefundlawyer.cooley.com/primer-management-fees-in-venture-capital-funds/ |url-status=live }}</ref>
|-
|Carried interest
|A share of the profits of the fund, typically 20%, paid to the fund's general partner as a performance incentive. The remaining 80% of the profits are allocated to the general partner and limited partners in proportion to their contributed capital.<ref>{{Cite web |title=Venture Capital: Financial and Tax Considerations |url=http://archives.cpajournal.com/2003/0803/dept/d086003.htm |access-date=2022-04-09 |website=archives.cpajournal.com |archive-date=August 10, 2022 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220810134752/http://archives.cpajournal.com/2003/0803/dept/d086003.htm |url-status=live }}</ref> Strong limited partner interest in top-tier venture firms has led to a general trend toward terms more favorable to the general partner, and certain groups are able to command a carried interest of 25 to 30% for their funds.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Silber |first=Jordan |date=2019-12-16 |title=Primer: Carried Interest in Venture Capital Funds |url=https://thefundlawyer.cooley.com/primer-carried-interest-in-venture-capital-funds/ |access-date=2022-04-09 |website=TheFundLawyer |language=en-US |archive-date=April 9, 2022 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220409080245/https://thefundlawyer.cooley.com/primer-carried-interest-in-venture-capital-funds/ |url-status=live }}</ref>
|}

Because a fund may run out of capital prior to the end of its life, larger venture capital firms usually have several overlapping funds at the same time; doing so lets the larger firm keep specialists in all stages of the development of firms almost constantly engaged. Smaller firms tend to thrive or fail with their initial industry contacts; by the time the fund cashes out, an entirely new generation of technologies and people is ascending, whom the general partners may not know well, and so it is prudent to reassess and shift industries or personnel rather than attempt to simply invest more in the industry or people the partners already know.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}}

===Alternatives===
{{More citations needed section|date=January 2021}}
Because of the strict requirements venture capitalists have for potential investments, many entrepreneurs seek [[seed funding]] from [[angel investors]], who may be more willing to invest in highly speculative opportunities, or may have a prior relationship with the entrepreneur.

Additionally, entrepreneurs may seek alternative financing, such as [[revenue-based financing]], to avoid giving up equity ownership in the business. For entrepreneurs seeking more than just funding, startup studios can be an appealing alternative to venture capitalists, as they provide operational support and an experienced team.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Kronenberger |first=Craig |date=2021-02-23 |title=How the Startup Studio Business Model Is Changing the Startup Economy as We Know It |url=https://medium.com/startup-studio-insider/how-the-startup-studio-business-model-is-changing-the-startup-economy-as-we-know-it-4a121a670007 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210428001951/https://medium.com/startup-studio-insider/how-the-startup-studio-business-model-is-changing-the-startup-economy-as-we-know-it-4a121a670007 |archive-date=April 28, 2021 |access-date=2021-03-16 |website=Medium |language=en}}</ref>

A growing number of companies are also turning to [[venture debt]] as an alternative, particularly in technology and life sciences sectors, where it provides working capital without significant equity dilution.<ref>{{Cite web |last=EY |date=2023-02-11 |title=Q3 2022 venture capital investment trends |url=https://www.ey.com/en_us/growth/venture-capital/q3-2022-venture-capital-investment-trends |access-date=2025-09-25 |website=EY |language=en-US}}</ref>

Furthermore, many venture capital firms will only seriously evaluate an investment in a [[start-up company]] otherwise unknown to them if the company can prove at least some of its claims about the technology and/or market potential for its product or services. To achieve this, or even just to avoid the dilutive effects of receiving funding before such claims are proven, many start-ups seek to self-finance [[sweat equity]] until they reach a point where they can credibly approach outside capital providers such as venture capitalists or [[angel investors]]. This practice is called "[[bootstrapping (business)|bootstrapping]]".

[[Equity crowdfunding]] is emerging as an alternative to traditional venture capital. Traditional [[crowdfunding]] is an approach to raising the capital required for a new project or enterprise by appealing to large numbers of ordinary people for small donations. While such an approach has long precedents in the sphere of charity, it is receiving renewed attention from entrepreneurs, now that social media and online communities make it possible to reach out to a group of potentially interested supporters at very low cost. Some [[equity crowdfunding]] models are also being applied specifically for startup funding, such as those listed at [[Comparison of crowd funding services]]. One of the reasons to look for alternatives to venture capital is the problem of the traditional VC model. The traditional VCs are shifting their focus to later-stage investments, and [[return on investment]] of many VC funds have been low or negative.<ref name="news.bbc.co.uk"/><ref>{{cite web |url = https://techcrunch.com/2010/02/15/grow-vc-launches-aiming-to-become-the-kiva-for-tech-startups/ |title = Grow VC launches, aiming to become the Kiva for tech startups |publisher = Eu.techcrunch.com |date = February 15, 2010 |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20120530064252/http://techcrunch.com/2010/02/15/grow-vc-launches-aiming-to-become-the-kiva-for-tech-startups/ |archive-date = May 30, 2012 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

In Europe and India, [[Media for equity]] is a partial alternative to venture capital funding. Media for equity investors are able to supply start-ups with often significant advertising campaigns in return for equity. In Europe, an investment advisory firm offers young ventures the option to exchange equity for services investment; their aim is to guide ventures through the development stage to arrive at a significant funding, mergers and acquisition, or other exit strategy.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.sollertis-strategy.com/index.html|title=Sollertis Strategy - Adventure Capitalist and Consulting Organisation|url-status=dead|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141229162850/http://www.sollertis-strategy.com/index.html|archive-date=December 29, 2014|df=mdy-all|access-date=December 29, 2014}}</ref>

In industries where assets can be [[Securitization|securitized]] effectively because they reliably generate future revenue streams or have a good potential for resale in case of [[foreclosure]], businesses may more cheaply be able to raise debt to finance their growth. Good examples would include asset-intensive extractive industries such as mining, or manufacturing industries. Offshore funding is provided via specialist venture capital trusts, which seek to use securitization in structuring hybrid multi-market transactions via an SPV ([[special purpose vehicle]]): a corporate entity that is designed solely for the purpose of the financing.

In addition to traditional venture capital and angel networks, groups have emerged, which allow groups of small investors or entrepreneurs themselves to compete in a privatized business plan competition where the group itself serves as the investor through a democratic process.<ref>{{cite web |url = http://growvc.com |title = Grow Venture Community |publisher = Growvc.com |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20100209014634/http://www.growvc.com/ |archive-date = February 9, 2010 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

Law firms are also increasingly acting as an intermediary between clients seeking venture capital and the firms providing it.<ref>{{cite web |last = Austin |first = Scott |url = https://www.wsj.com/articles/SB10001424052748703453804575479650072184066 |title = "Law Firms Offer Discounts, Play Matchmaker," The Wall Street Journal, Sept. 9, 2010 |publisher = Online.wsj.com |date = September 9, 2010 |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20150115040929/http://www.wsj.com/articles/SB10001424052748703453804575479650072184066 |archive-date = January 15, 2015 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

Other forms include venture resources that seek to provide non-monetary support to launch a new venture.

==Role in employment==
Every year, there are nearly 2 million businesses created in the US, but only 600–800 get venture capital funding.<ref>{{cite web|title=Venture Impact: The Economic Importance of Venture-Backed Companies to the U.S. Economy|url=http://www.nvca.org/index.php?option=com_content&view=article&id=255&Itemid=103|url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140630170428/http://www.nvca.org/index.php?option=com_content&view=article&id=255&Itemid=103|archive-date=June 30, 2014|access-date=May 18, 2012|publisher=National Venture Capital Association|df=mdy-all}}</ref> According to the National Venture Capital Association, 11% of private sector jobs come from venture-backed companies and venture-backed revenue accounts for 21% of US GDP.<ref>{{cite book |url = http://www.nvca.org/index.php?option=com_content&view=article&id=255&Itemid=103 |publisher = [[IHS Global Insight]] |title = Venture Impact |edition = 5 |page = 2 |isbn = 978-0-9785015-7-0 |year = 2009 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20140630170428/http://www.nvca.org/index.php?option=com_content&view=article&id=255&Itemid=103 |archive-date = June 30, 2014 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

==Gender disparities==
In 2020, female-founded companies raised 2.8% of capital investment from venture capital, the highest amount recorded.<ref>{{Cite news|last1=Bittner|first1=Ashley|last2=Lau|first2=Brigette|date=2021-02-25|title=Women-Led Startups Received Just 2.3% of VC Funding in 2020|work=Harvard Business Review|url=https://hbr.org/2021/02/women-led-startups-received-just-2-3-of-vc-funding-in-2020|access-date=2021-10-26|issn=0017-8012|archive-date=October 26, 2021|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20211026085152/https://hbr.org/2021/02/women-led-startups-received-just-2-3-of-vc-funding-in-2020|url-status=live}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|title=US VC investment in female founders hits all-time high|url=https://techcrunch.com/2019/12/09/us-vc-investment-in-female-founders-hits-all-time-high/|access-date=2021-10-26|website=TechCrunch|date=December 9, 2019 |language=en-US}}</ref> [[Babson College]]'s Diana Report found that the number of women [[partner (business rank)|partner]]s in VC firms decreased from 10% in 1999 to 6% in 2014. The report also found that 97% of VC-funded businesses had male [[chief executive]]s, and that businesses with all-male teams were more than four times as likely to receive VC funding compared to teams with at least one woman.<ref name="diana-report">{{cite web|url=http://www.babson.edu/Academics/centers/blank-center/global-research/diana/Documents/diana-project-executive-summary-2014.pdf|title=Diana Report|first=Candida|last=Brush|publisher=Women Entrepreneurs 2014|year=2014|access-date=2016-01-14|url-status=dead|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160122183718/http://www.babson.edu/Academics/centers/blank-center/global-research/diana/Documents/diana-project-executive-summary-2014.pdf|archive-date=January 22, 2016|df=mdy-all}}</ref> However, analysis also shows tiny percentage of companies with women CEOs are receiving venture capital, these businesses are receiving approximately the same level of investment, and are just as likely to receive early- and late-stage funding, as those with men CEOs. Furthermore, there is evidence that they perform just as well as all-male firms once approved.<ref name="diana-report" />

Currently, about 3% of all venture capital is going to woman-led companies. More than 75% of VC firms in the US did not have any female venture capitalists at the time they were surveyed.<ref name="gompers">{{cite magazine|url=https://www.wired.com/2015/03/gender-problem-venture-capital-really-really-bad/|title=The gender problem in venture capital is really, really bad|first=Davey|last=Alba|author-link=Davey Alba|magazine=Wired|date=2015-03-20|access-date=2016-01-15|url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160210092058/http://www.wired.com/2015/03/gender-problem-venture-capital-really-really-bad/|archive-date=February 10, 2016|df=mdy-all}}</ref> It was found that a greater fraction of VC firms had never had a woman represent them on the board of one of their [[Investment portfolio|portfolio companies]]. For comparison, a UC Davis study focusing on large public companies in California found 49.5% with at least one female [[board of directors|board seat]].<ref name="Ellis2006">{{cite web |last=Ellis |first=Katrina |year=2006 |title=UC Davis Study of California Women Business Leaders |url=http://gsm.ucdavis.edu/sites/main/files/file-attachments/ucdaviswomenstudyfull.pdf |publisher=UC Regents |access-date=March 25, 2015 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150402165726/http://gsm.ucdavis.edu/sites/main/files/file-attachments/ucdaviswomenstudyfull.pdf |archive-date=April 2, 2015 |df=mdy-all }}</ref>

==Geographical differences==
Venture capital, as an industry, originated in the United States, and American firms have traditionally been the largest participants in venture deals with the bulk of venture capital being deployed in American companies. However, increasingly, non-US venture investment is growing, and the number and size of non-US venture capitalists have been expanding.<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Leonard |first1=Alice |last2=Roongta |first2=Rishi |last3=Gomatam |first3=Arun |date=2025-08-08 |title=Global Venture Capital Outlook: The Latest Trends |url=https://www.bain.com/insights/global-venture-capital-outlook-latest-trends-snap-chart/ |access-date=2025-08-21 |website=Bain |language=en}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Cuantico |date=2025-03-05 |title=Latin America Venture Capital Report 2025 |url=https://cuanticovc.com/latin-america-venture-capital-report-2025/ |access-date=2025-08-21 |website=Cuantico VC |language=en-US}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last1=Sheth |first1=Arpan |last2=Krishnan |first2=Sriwatsan |last3=Addepalli |first3=Prabhav Kashyap |last4=Muralidhar |first4=Aditya |last5=Arya |first5=Prerna |date=2025-03-13 |title=India Venture Capital Report 2025 |url=https://www.bain.com/insights/india-venture-capital-report-2025/ |access-date=2025-08-21 |website=Bain |language=en}}</ref>

Venture capital has been used as a tool for [[economic development]] in a variety of developing regions. In many of these regions, with less developed financial sectors, venture capital plays a role in facilitating [[access to finance]] for [[small and medium enterprises]] (SMEs), which in most cases would not qualify for receiving bank loans.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}}

In the year of 2008, while VC funding was still majorly dominated by U.S. money ($28.8 billion invested in over 2550 deals in 2008), compared to international fund investments ($13.4 billion invested elsewhere), there has been an average 5% growth in the venture capital deals outside the US, mainly in [[China]] and [[Europe]].<ref name="venturebeat.com">{{cite news |url = https://venturebeat.com/2009/02/18/international-venture-funding-rose-15-percent-in-2008/ |title = International venture funding rose 5 percent in 2008 |work = VentureBeat |date = February 18, 2009 |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20120714021930/http://venturebeat.com/2009/02/18/international-venture-funding-rose-15-percent-in-2008/ |archive-date = July 14, 2012 |df = mdy-all }}</ref> Geographical differences can be significant. For instance, in the UK, 4% of British investment goes to venture capital, compared to about 33% in the U.S.<ref>{{cite web |url = http://www.briskfox.com/open/years/2009_q1/do_v_c44780.php |title = Mandelson, Peter. "There is no Google, or Amazon, or Microsoft or Apple in the UK, Mandelson tells BVCA." BriskFox Financial News, March 11, 2009 |publisher = Briskfox.com |access-date = May 18, 2012 |url-status = live |archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20120120235413/http://www.briskfox.com/open/years/2009_q1/do_v_c44780.php |archive-date = January 20, 2012 |df = mdy-all }}</ref>

VC funding has been shown to be positively related to a country's [[individualistic culture]].<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Gantenbein |first1=Pascal |last2=Kind |first2=Axel |last3=Volonte|first3=Christophe |title=Individualism and Venture Capital: A Cross-Country Study |journal=Management International Review |volume=59 |issue=5 |pages=741–777 |date=2019 |doi=10.1007/s11575-019-00394-7 |doi-access=free }}</ref> According to economist Jeffrey Funk, however, more than 90% of US startups valued over $1 billion lost money between 2019–2020 and return on investment from VC barely exceed return from public stock markets over the last 25 years.<ref>{{Cite web|date=2021-02-20|title=The Crisis of Venture Capital: Fixing America's Broken Start-Up System|url=https://americanaffairsjournal.org/2021/02/the-crisis-of-venture-capital-fixing-americas-broken-start-up-system/|access-date=2021-06-16|website=American Affairs Journal|language=en-US|archive-date=June 17, 2021|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210617090959/https://americanaffairsjournal.org/2021/02/the-crisis-of-venture-capital-fixing-americas-broken-start-up-system/|url-status=live}}</ref>

===United States===
[[File:US VC funding.png|thumb|Quarterly U.S. Venture Capital Investments, 1995–2017|alt=Quarterly U.S. venture capital investments between 1995 and 2017]]
[[File:Venture capital by area history.png|thumb|Venture capital investment by area|alt=Venture capital by area]]
[[File:Venture capital by state.png|thumb|Venture capital by state, 2016|alt=Venture capital by state as of 2016]]In the [[United States]], venture capital investment reached $321.6 billion in 2025, marking the highest-grossing year on record. Early data from KPMG indicates that 2026 is on pace to set a new record, with the first quarter alone raising $267 billion. This surge is primarily driven by massive investments and soaring valuations in the artificial intelligence (AI) sector.<ref>{{Cite web |date=April 15, 2026 |year=2026 |title=Q1'26 Venture Pulse Report — Global trends |url=https://kpmg.com/xx/en/what-we-do/industries/private-enterprise/venture-pulse.html |archive-url= |access-date=May 19, 2026 |website=KPMG |language=en}}</ref>
===Canada===
Canadian technology companies have attracted interest from the global venture capital community partially as a result of generous tax incentive through the [[Scientific Research and Experimental Development Tax Credit Program|Scientific Research and Experimental Development]] (SR&ED) investment tax credit program.{{Citation needed|date=November 2010}} The basic incentive available to any Canadian corporation performing R&D is a refundable tax credit that is equal to 20% of "qualifying" R&D expenditures (labour, material, R&D contracts, and R&D equipment). An enhanced 35% refundable tax credit of available to certain (i.e. small) Canadian-controlled private corporations (CCPCs). Because the CCPC rules require a minimum of 50% Canadian ownership in the company performing R&D, foreign investors who would like to benefit from the larger 35% tax credit must accept minority position in the company, which might not be desirable. The SR&ED program does not restrict the export of any technology or intellectual property that may have been developed with the benefit of SR&ED tax incentives.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Agency |first=Canada Revenue |date=2023-04-13 |title=Scientific Research and Experimental Development (SR&ED) tax incentives |url=https://www.canada.ca/en/revenue-agency/services/scientific-research-experimental-development-tax-incentive-program.html |access-date=2025-08-03 |website=www.canada.ca}}</ref>

Canada also has a fairly unusual form of venture capital generation in its [[Labour-sponsored venture capital corporation|labour-sponsored venture capital corporations (LSVCC)]]. These funds, also known as Retail Venture Capital or Labour Sponsored Investment Funds (LSIF), are generally sponsored by labor unions and offer [[tax break]]s from government to encourage retail investors to purchase the funds. Generally, these Retail Venture Capital funds only invest in companies where the majority of employees are in Canada. However, innovative structures have been developed to permit LSVCCs to direct in Canadian subsidiaries of corporations incorporated in jurisdictions outside of Canada.{{Citation needed|date=January 2021}} In 2022, the [[Information and Communications Technology (ICT)]] sector closed around 50% of Canada's venture capital deals, 16% were in the [[List of life sciences|Life Sciences]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Mandel |first=Charles |date=2023-02-22 |title=Despite market conditions, 2022 second-highest year on record for Canadian venture capital |url=https://betakit.com/despite-market-conditions-2022-second-highest-year-on-record-for-canadian-venture-capital/ |access-date=2023-12-31 |website=Betakit |archive-date=December 31, 2023 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20231231150740/https://betakit.com/despite-market-conditions-2022-second-highest-year-on-record-for-canadian-venture-capital/ |url-status=live }}</ref>

=== Australia and New Zealand ===
In Australia and New Zealand, there have been 3 waves of VC, starting with Bill Ferris who founded IVC in 1970. are more than one hundred active VC funds, syndicates, or angel investors making VC-style investments. The 2nd wave was led by Starfish & Southern Cross VC, with the latter producing the leading VC of the 3rd wave, Blackbird.<ref>{{Cite book |last1=Marshall |first1=Dr Larry |url=https://ebooks.publish.csiro.au/content/invention-innovation |title=Invention to Innovation |last2=Daroczy |first2=Jenna |date=2023 |publisher=CSIRO Publishing |isbn=978-1-4863-1638-0 |language=en |doi=10.1071/9781486316380}}</ref> There was a boom in 2018, and today there are more than one hundred active VC funds, syndicates, or angel investors making VC-style investments. There have been few [[Nasdaq|Nasdaq IPOs]] of Australian VC backed startups, with only Looksmart<ref>{{Cite web |date=1999-06-16 |title=LookSmart takes the big leap |url=https://www.afr.com/politics/looksmart-takes-the-big-leap-19990616-jl8br |access-date=2024-06-03 |website=Australian Financial Review |language=en}}</ref> from [[Bill Ferris]]'s fund, and Quantenna<ref>{{Cite web |last=Jones |first=Donovan |date=2016-10-02 |title=Quantenna Communications: This $100 Million IPO Is A Video And IoE Play (NASDAQ:QTNA-DEFUNCT-569984) {{!}} Seeking Alpha |url=https://seekingalpha.com/article/4009594-quantenna-communications-this-100-million-ipo-is-video-and-ioe-play |access-date=2024-06-03 |website=seekingalpha.com |language=en}}</ref> from [[Larry R. Marshall|Larry Marshall]]'s Southern Cross VC, but Blackbird is expected to IPO [[Canva]] soon.<ref>{{Cite web | url=https://www.afr.com/technology/canva-co-founder-says-ipo-more-appealing-as-valuations-rise-20250905-p5msnm | title=Canva co-founder says IPO 'more appealing' as valuations rise | date=September 5, 2025 | access-date=October 22, 2025 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20250915041745/https://www.afr.com/technology/canva-co-founder-says-ipo-more-appealing-as-valuations-rise-20250905-p5msnm | archive-date=September 15, 2025 | url-status=live }}</ref>

The State of Startup Funding report found that in 2021, over AUD $10 billion AUD was invested into Australian and New Zealand startups across 682 deals. This represents a 3x increase from the $3.1 billion that was invested in 2020.<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.smh.com.au/technology/10-billion-flows-to-australian-start-ups-in-banner-year-20220228-p5a0bb.html | title=$10 billion flows to Australian start-ups in banner year | date=February 28, 2022 | access-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220621020657/https://www.smh.com.au/technology/10-billion-flows-to-australian-start-ups-in-banner-year-20220228-p5a0bb.html | url-status=live }}</ref>

Some notable Australian and New Zealand startup success stories include graphic design company [[Canva]],<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.forbes.com/sites/alexkonrad/2021/09/14/canva-raises-at-40-billion-valuation-and-founders-pledge-away-their-wealth/ | title=Canva Raises at $40 Billion Valuation — itItsounders Are Pledging Away Most of Their Wealth | website=[[Forbes]] | access-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-date=September 20, 2021 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210920005230/https://www.forbes.com/sites/alexkonrad/2021/09/14/canva-raises-at-40-billion-valuation-and-founders-pledge-away-their-wealth/ | url-status=live }}</ref> financial services provider [[Airwallex]], New Zealand payments provider Vend (acquired by Lightspeed), rent-to-buy company [[OwnHome]],<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.afr.com/technology/cba-backs-live-now-buy-later-start-up-that-will-ditch-home-deposits-20220203-p59tix | title=CBA backs 'live now, buy later' start-up that will ditch home deposits | date=February 3, 2022 | access-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220621023705/https://www.afr.com/technology/cba-backs-live-now-buy-later-start-up-that-will-ditch-home-deposits-20220203-p59tix | url-status=live }}</ref> and direct-to-consumer propositions such as [[Eucalyptus Health|Eucalyptus]] (a house of direct-to-consumer telehealth brands), and [[Lyka (company)|Lyka]] (a pet wellness company).<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.afr.com/street-talk/hungry-investors-buy-into-pet-food-start-up-lyka-20210705-p5870h | title=Hungry investors buy into pet food start-up Lyka | date=July 5, 2021 | access-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220621023209/https://www.afr.com/street-talk/hungry-investors-buy-into-pet-food-start-up-lyka-20210705-p5870h | url-status=live }}</ref> BugHerd, the developer of a website feedback and bug tracking software application<ref>{{cite web |url=https://www.smh.com.au/technology/aussie-tech-startup-bugherd-strikes-gold-raising-500k-20120124-1qeyd.html |title=Aussie tech startup BugHerd strikes gold, raising $500k |author=Asher Moses |website=The Sydney Morning Herald |date=2012-01-24 |access-date=2026-03-30}}</ref> joined the Startmate accelerator in 2011 and raised a $1.5 million between 2012 and 2014, mostly from Tankstream Ventures<ref>{{cite web |date=2014-08-26 |title=BugHerd raises $1 million for task management system rollout |url=https://www.smartcompany.com.au/finance/bugherd-raises-1-million-for-task-management-system-rollout |access-date=2026-03-30 |website=SmartCompany}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |author=Georgia Wilkins |date=2014-08-25 |title=Ample VC available for Aussie startups as BugHerd expands |url=https://www.afr.com/technology/ample-vc-available-for-aussie-startups-as-bugherd-expands-20140825-jcy45 |access-date=2026-03-30 |website=Financial Review}}</ref> and Starfish Ventures.<ref>{{cite web |url=https://delimiter.com.au/2012/01/25/bugherd-takes-500k-venture-capital-investment/|title=BugHerd takes $500k venture capital investment|author=Renai LeMay|website=Delimiter|date=2012-01-25|access-date=2026-03-30}}</ref>

In 2022, the largest Australian funds are [[Blackbird Ventures]], [[Square Peg Capital]], and [[Airtree Ventures]]. These three funds have more than $1 billion AUD under management across multiple funds. These funds have funding from institutional capital, including AustralianSuper and Hostplus, family offices, and sophisticated individual high-net-wealth investors.<ref>{{cite web | url=https://www.afr.com/street-talk/blackbird-targets-1b-vc-fund-square-peg-airtree-also-ready-to-raise-20211125-p59c6s | title=Blackbird targets $1b VC fund; Square Peg, AirTree also ready to raise | date=November 25, 2021 | access-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-date=June 21, 2022 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220621020657/https://www.afr.com/street-talk/blackbird-targets-1b-vc-fund-square-peg-airtree-also-ready-to-raise-20211125-p59c6s | url-status=live }}</ref>
